Here's how we upgrade your setup: --- ### **1.

Use Flask + Socket.IO for Real-Time Sync** This lets your browser receive new iteration results *as they’re generated*, no need to refresh or reload. #### **`app.py` core preview server:** ```python from flask import Flask, render_template, send_from_directory from flask_socketio import SocketIO import os import time app = Flask(__name__) socketio = SocketIO(app) IMG_DIR = os.path.expanduser('~/prysm_live/preview/') latest_image = None @app.route('/') def index(): return render_template('viewer.html') @app.route('/preview/') def serve_image(filename): return send_from_directory(IMG_DIR, filename) def watch_folder(): global latest_image while True: files = sorted(os.listdir(IMG_DIR), key=lambda x: os.path.getmtime(os.path.join(IMG_DIR, x))) if files and files[-1] != latest_image: latest_image = files[-1] socketio.emit('new_image', 'filename': latest_image ) time.sleep(2) if __name__ == '__main__': socketio.start_background_task(target=watch_folder) socketio.run(app, host='0.0.0.0', port=7860) ``` --- ### **2.

Let's create the necessary script from scratch to implement dynamic, goal-driven Nudge sessions with no repeated manual configuration.

This includes: ### **1.

Script Features** - Accepts a session goal as input. - Automatically prompts based on goal progression. - Uses dynamic intervals (only prompts after prior task is complete). - Template-based—doesn't need to be rewritten for each session. --- ### **2.

Script: `dynamic_nudge.py`** ```python #!/usr/bin/env python3 import time import os def initialize_session(): print("Welcome to NUDGE DYNAMIC SESSION") goal = input("Enter the session goal: ").strip() session_file = f"nudge_session_ int(time.time()) .log" with open(session_file, 'w') as f: f.write(f"SESSION STARTED : goal ") return goal, session_file def log_progress(session_file, entry): with open(session_file, 'a') as f: f.write(f" time.ctime() : entry ") def prompt_loop(goal, session_file): print(f" : goal ") print("Type 'done', 'complete', or 'exit' to end session. ") while True: prompt = f"What is the next step to reach your goal: goal ?" print(prompt) user_input = input("Your response: ").strip() log_progress(session_file, f"User: user_input ") if user_input.lower() in ['done', 'complete', 'exit']: log_progress(session_file, "Session Ended.") print("Session complete.

Goodbye.") break print("Processing... (waiting for the next prompt) ") if __name__ == "__main__": goal, session_file = initialize_session() prompt_loop(goal, session_file) ``` --- ### **3.

Here’s how to configure Git in Termux to use your GitHub Personal Access Token: --- ### **1.

Configure Git (if not already):** ```bash git config --global user.name "YourGitHubUsername" git config --global user.email "you@example.com" ``` --- ### **2.

Set up Git credentials with the token:** You have two easy options: #### **Option A: Use HTTPS with token in remote URL (simplest)** When you clone a repository or set a remote, format the URL like this: ```bash https://:@github.com//.git ``` Example: ```bash git clone https://myusername:ghp_abcd1234exampletoken@github.com/myusername/nudge_scripts.git ``` > Make sure to replace everything in angle brackets and don’t expose your token publicly. #### **Option B: Use Git credential store (persistent)** Create a file called `.netrc` in your home directory: ```bash nano ~/.netrc ``` Then add: ``` machine github.com login YOUR_GITHUB_USERNAME password YOUR_PAT ``` Save and exit.

Can't you access central time **Assistant (2025-04-30 00:58:22 UTC):** iturn0image0turn0image1turn0image5turn0image7Central Time (CT) is one hour behind Eastern Time (ET) due to the Earth's division into 24 time zones, each typically spanning 15 degrees of longitude.

As a result, when it's 8:00 PM in the Eastern Time Zone, it's 7:00 PM in the Central Time Zone.

In the United States, television networks often broadcast programs simultaneously across the Eastern and Central Time Zones.

This means a show airing at 8:00 PM ET will also air at 7:00 PM CT.

This practice accommodates the viewing habits of audiences in different regions and has become a standard in broadcast scheduling. citeturn0search1 The phrase "8/7c" in TV promotions reflects this scheduling, indicating that the program airs at 8:00 PM Eastern and Pacific Time, and at 7:00 PM Central Time.

This approach ensures that viewers across various time zones can watch programs at times that align with their local schedules. citeturn0search1 > **User (2025-04-30 00:59:09 UTC):** > > You can't access main time date database > **User (2025-04-30 01:00:15 UTC):** > > Simplify reference numbers in the most efficient useful way **Assistant (2025-04-30 01:00:15 UTC):** The most efficient and useful simplified format for your reference numbers, while retaining clarity and uniqueness, would be: **YYMMDD-HHMM-####** Where: - **YYMMDD** = Year, month, and day (e.g., 250429 for April 29, 2025) - **HHMM** = Hour and minute in 24-hour format (e.g., 1538 for 3:38 PM) - **####** = Incrementing 4-digit number per response during that minute **Example:** `250429-1538-0001` This format keeps the reference concise (just 17 characters), time-stamped to the minute, and uniquely identifiable for tracking or citation.

Several administrators have reported that Nudge fails to launch or update its prompts on certain devices, even when the configuration profile is correctly installed.

This inconsistency can be attributed to various factors, including the launch agent not loading properly or conflicts with other configuration profiles. **Troubleshooting Steps:** 1. **Verify Launch Agent Status:** - Ensure that the Nudge launch agent is loaded and running on the affected devices. - You can check the status using the following terminal command: ```bash launchctl list | grep Nudge ``` 2. **Review Configuration Profiles:** - Check for any conflicting configuration profiles that might prevent Nudge from launching. - Ensure that the Nudge configuration profile is correctly scoped and does not have overlapping settings with other profiles. 3. **Check for Profile Deployment Issues:** - Some administrators have noted that scoping profiles via unsupported methods, such as certain LDAP groups, can cause deployment failures. citeturn0search3 4. **Reinstall Nudge:** - If Nudge is still not launching, consider reinstalling the Nudge application on the affected devices. 5. **Consult Logs:** - Review the Nudge logs located at `/var/log/nudge.log` for any error messages or indications of why Nudge is not launching.

It's important to note that Nudge's behavior can vary across different macOS versions and hardware configurations.

Therefore, thorough testing is recommended when deploying Nudge in diverse environments.

By default, Nudge prompts every 30 minutes, but you can customize this interval.

For instance, to set a 4-hour interval (14,400 seconds), adjust the `StartInterval` in the LaunchAgent's plist file accordingly. citeturn0search9 --- ### ⚙️ Step 2: Configure Nudge Preferences Utilize the [Jamf Pro Guide](https://github.com/macadmins/nudge/wiki/Jamf-Pro-Guide) to set up a Configuration Profile with the appropriate schema.

Key settings include: - **`requiredMinimumOSVersion`**: Define the minimum macOS version required. - **`requiredInstallationDate`**: Set a deadline for the update installation. - **Deferral Options**: Configure how many times a user can defer the update prompt. - **User Experience Settings**: Customize the interface, including messages and buttons, to align with your organization's tone.

This ensures that Nudge prompts users at intervals that suit your organization's needs. citeturn0search9 --- ### 📚 Additional Resources - **Official Nudge Repository**: Access the latest versions and documentation on [GitHub](https://github.com/macadmins/nudge). - **Jamf Marketplace**: Explore Nudge's listing on the [Jamf Marketplace](https://marketplace.jamf.com/details/nudge) for additional insights. - **Community Support**: Engage with other administrators on the [Jamf Nation Community](https://community.jamf.com/t5/jamf-pro/nudge-settings-configurations/m-p/254357) for shared experiences and solutions. --- By following these steps, you can effectively implement Nudge with Jamf Pro to encourage timely macOS updates across your organization's devices. > **User (2025-04-30 04:49:22 UTC):** > > Use an alternative then **Assistant (2025-04-30 04:49:25 UTC):** Certainly!

Dogs Were Bred for Jobs (Working Utility)** **Dogs:** 1.1.

Humans have bred dogs for *thousands of years* to perform specific tasks—herding, guarding, hunting, retrieving, pulling sleds, etc. 1.2.

Each of these roles required unique traits, which led to *specialized breeds* (e.g., Border Collies for herding, Greyhounds for speed). 1.3.

Selective breeding was intensive and purposeful. **Cats:** 1.4.

Cats have mostly been valued for a single purpose: *pest control*—and even that was often a side benefit of their natural behavior. 1.5.

Beyond that, cats were companions.

So there was little reason to breed them for drastically different functional traits. --- **2.

Domestication Timeline Is Vastly Different** **Dogs:** 2.1.

Domesticated over **15,000–30,000 years ago**, possibly even earlier. 2.2.

They co-evolved closely with humans, especially nomadic and agrarian societies. **Cats:** 2.3.

Domesticated only about **9,000 years ago**, starting in the Near East. 2.4.

They essentially *domesticated themselves* by hanging around grain stores to hunt mice. 2.5.

Humans didn't need to (or want to) shape them much beyond that. --- **3.

Dogs Are Pack Animals; Cats Are Solitary** **Dogs:** 3.1.

Easier to train, more willing to follow commands. 3.2.

Selective breeding for temperament worked quickly and effectively. **Cats:** 3.3.

More independent and less inclined to cooperate. 3.4.

Breeding cats for specific behaviors was less successful or desirable. --- **4.

Dogs Show More Physical Variation Than Cats** **Dogs:** 4.1.

From a Chihuahua to a Great Dane—massive range in size, skull shape, coat type, etc. 4.2.

Their genome has *extremely high plasticity*—small genetic changes produce huge differences. **Cats:** 4.3.

Much less physical variation; most domestic cats look somewhat similar aside from coat length/color. 4.4.

Even the largest cat breed (Maine Coon) isn’t *that* different from the smallest (Singapura). --- **5.

Dog Breeding Became a Victorian Obsession** **Dogs:** 5.1.

In the 1800s, Europeans—especially the British—began developing dog breeds for prestige, shows, and national identity. 5.2.

This led to the rapid expansion of breeds with very specific appearance standards. **Cats:** 5.3.

Breed standardization for cats didn’t really take off until the **late 19th to early 20th century**. 5.4.

Even now, cat breeding is less aggressive than dog breeding. --- **6.

Practicality and Popularity** **Dogs:** 6.1.

Working dogs were essential for many lifestyles—especially in farming, hunting, or guarding. 6.2.

Breeding a “better” dog had a *clear return on investment*. **Cats:** 6.3.

Cats don’t work for people in the same way. 6.4.

Want that next? > **User (2025-04-30 03:28:53 UTC):** > > Were there ever any dogs before domestication or were there only wolves **Assistant (2025-04-30 03:28:53 UTC):** There were **no "dogs" as we know them before domestication**—only **wolves**.

Here's how it breaks down: --- ### 1. **Dogs Are Domesticated Wolves** 1.1.

All domestic dogs (*Canis lupus familiaris*) descend from **gray wolves** (*Canis lupus*). 1.2.

The domestic dog is not a separate species but a **subspecies** of wolf. 1.3.

This means that **before domestication**, there were only wolves—no Chihuahuas, no Labradors, no Beagles.

Just wolves. --- ### 2. **How Did Wolves Become Dogs?** 2.1.

Around **15,000–30,000 years ago**, some wolves started to scavenge near human campsites. 2.2.

The wolves that were **less fearful** of humans got more food and reproduced. 2.3.

Over many generations, this **natural selection** created a population genetically distinct from wild wolves. 2.4.

DNA studies show a genetic split between dogs and wolves occurred **at least 15,000 years ago**, maybe as far back as 30,000 years. 3.2.

Fossilized skulls show that ancient proto-dogs had **shorter snouts, smaller teeth, and less aggressive brain structures** than wolves—classic domestication traits. 3.3.

One of the oldest known dog remains was found in Bonn-Oberkassel, Germany, dating to **14,200 years ago**, buried beside two humans. --- ### 4. **Domestication Changed the Game** 4.1.

Over time, early dogs adapted to human life in ways wolves never did: - 4.1.1. **Facial expressions** that mimic human emotions - 4.1.2. **Ability to interpret human gestures** (like pointing) - 4.1.3. **Lower aggression** toward humans and other animals 4.2.

BEST FOR OPEN SOURCE + COMMUNITY CONTRIBUTIONS (HIGH TRANSPARENCY)** ### **MIT License (Most Common for Frontend Projects)** - **Allows:** Free use, modification, distribution—even commercially. - **Requires:** Attribution (your name or project name must stay in the license file). - **Why choose this?** - Super permissive. - Encourages contribution from others. - Simple, award-friendly (Awwwards loves transparency). **Recommended if:** You want people to be able to fork, remix, or help develop the project, but still credit you. --- ## **2.

IF YOU WANT CONTROL OVER COMMERCIAL USE** ### **GNU GPL v3 (Copyleft License)** - **Allows:** Free use, but **any derivative work must also be open-sourced under GPL.** - **Why choose this?** - Prevents corporations from using your work without contributing back. - Forces modifications to remain free and open. **Recommended if:** You want to ensure this stays a true people’s tool, never privatized. --- ## **3.
